=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return 'counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word) + 1
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

```python
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return 'counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word) + 1
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

```
int n = word.size(), ans = 0, right = n;
for (int left = n - 1; left >= 0; --left) {
    int node = 0;
```

Let's design fixed code:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_len(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            L = forbidden_len(i, j)
            if L:
                j = i + L - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Let's examine original algorithm more deeply. The original code with j=n and isForbidden(s) returning length of first forbidden prefix is correct? We tested. But there is a subtle bug: `isForbidden` returns `counter` as soon as `"end" in t`. Suppose `s` starts with a forbidden word that is a prefix of another forbidden word. It returns the shorter length. That's correct as argued. But what if `s` starts with a path that doesn't end but later a forbidden word appears not at start? It returns False. The algorithm relies on detecting forbidden words when their start index is i. But if j has been moved to before that start, then the forbidden word is not included in any window for earlier starts? Let's prove.

We maintain invariant: For current i, `j` is the smallest index such that no forbidden word is fully contained in `word[i:j]`? Actually after processing i+1..n-1, j is some boundary. For current i, we check if there is a forbidden word starting at i within `[i, j)`. If yes, set j to its last index, so `word[i:j]` excludes that forbidden word. But what if there is a forbidden word starting at i that is longer than the first prefix found? We don't need to consider longer because if the first prefix is forbidden, any substring containing it is invalid; to make a valid substring starting at i, we must end before the end of that prefix. If we end before the end of the first prefix, we also exclude any longer forbidden word starting at i because longer includes the first prefix and extends beyond. So okay.

What if there is a forbidden word starting at i that is not a prefix of the trie path? It is a prefix by definition. If `c not in t` at some point, no forbidden word starting at i can match because all forbidden words share prefix. So return False.

What if there is a forbidden word starting at i that ends after j? Since j is exclusive boundary, if its start is i and j <= i, no non-empty substring. If j > i, we only traverse up to j-1. If the forbidden word extends beyond j, then it is not fully contained in `word[i:j]`, so no need to update j. But could it be fully contained in a window starting earlier than i and ending at some later index? Wait j is the exclusive right boundary for all future starts (which are < i). If a forbidden word starts at i and extends beyond j, then for any earlier start s < i, the window `[s, j)` ends before the forbidden word's end, so it cannot contain the full forbidden word because the forbidden word starts at i >= s but ends beyond j. Thus safe. If later, when processing earlier starts, j might increase? No, j only decreases (or stays). So the forbidden word remains excluded. Good.

What about a forbidden word starting before i and ending after j? It would have been detected when processing its start, if that start >= current? Actually processing goes from right to left. Suppose forbidden word starts at s < i and ends at e. When we processed i? We process decreasing i. At i = s (later in loop, since s < current i? Wait loop from n-1 down to 0. If current i is some value, we have already processed all starts > i. A forbidden word starting at s < i will be processed later. At that time, j may be <= e? It will be detected and j updated. Good.

Need maybe consider if `j` can become less than i? In code, if L = 1, j = i + 1 - 1 = i. Then res = j - i = 0. For next i-1, forbidden_len(i-1, j=i) checks range(i-1, i), one char. Good. If L = 0, j remains > i. So j >= i always? Initially j=n >= i. If L found, j = i+L-1. Since L>=1, j >= i. So j-i >=0. Good.

Let's search memory of LeetCode 2781. The official solution uses trie and sliding window:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        forbidden_set = set(forbidden)
        left = 0
        ans = 0
        for right in range(len(word)):
            for i in range(right, max(right - 10, left - 1), -1):
                if word[i:right+1] in forbidden_set:
                    left = i + 1
                    break
            ans = max(ans, right - left + 1)
        return ans
```
This uses set and max length 10. Another trie solution:
```

left = 0
for right, c in enumerate(word):
    node = trie
    for i in range(right, max(right-10, left-1), -1):
        if word[i] not in node: break
        node = node[word[i]]
        if "end" in node:
            left = i+1
            break
    ans = max(ans, right-left+1)
```

The provided code seems from a reverse iteration solution. Let's see if the original code's algorithm with `isForbidden(word[i:j])` and `j = i + truc - 1` is exactly a known solution? I recall a LeetCode solution:
```
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            node = trie
            for ch in f:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
            node['#'] = True

        def find(s):
            node = trie
            for i, ch in enumerate(s):
                if ch not in node:
                    return False
                node = node[ch]
                if '#' in node:
                    return i + 1
            return False

        ans = 0
        r = len(word)
        for l in range(len(word)-1, -1, -1):
            length = find(word[l:r])
            if length:
                r = l + length - 1
            ans = max(ans, r - l)
        return ans
```
This is a known Python solution? It uses slicing but maybe accepted due max forbidden length 10? Wait slicing word[l:r] length can be up to n, but find returns early after max forbidden length? Actually find traverses until either no path or end. If no forbidden prefix, it may traverse the entire slice if all characters are in trie path but no end. But since forbidden words max length 10, the trie depth is at most 10. If after 10 chars there is no end and no child? Actually if c not in node, return False. If all chars in trie path for more than 10? Trie depth max 10, so after at most 10 chars, there is no child for next char? Wait if forbidden words max length 10, trie depth is 10. If word has a long prefix that matches a path of length 10 but no end, then at char 11, c not in node (because node at depth 10 has no children except maybe sentinel), so return False. Thus find traverses at most maxLen+1, not length of slice. Slicing itself creates a string of length up to n, O(n) per iteration, so still O(n^2) due slicing. But maybe in Python slicing with step? It copies. So TLE for n=1e5. But maybe LeetCode accepted? Let's check: The known solution with slicing might pass because `word[l:r]` creates substring of length up to n-l, but r is often small? Let's decide final code. I think we should output efficient fixed code:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_len(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            L = forbidden_len(i, j)
            if L:
                j = i + L - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Let's maybe produce code that closely follows original but avoids slicing:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start, end):
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

This keeps function name `isForbidden` and variable `truc`. It returns 0 instead of False. Good.

Let's write final code with `isForbidden`:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Need maybe consider if `isForbidden` returns 0 for no forbidden, but original returned False. In `if truc`, 0 false. Good.

Potential issue: If a forbidden word has length 0, `isForbidden` returns 0, so not detected. But constraints no. If we want to be extra robust, we could add:
```
if "" in forbidden:
    return 0
```
But that would be a behavior change for invalid input. Not needed.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word with length 0 and we don't handle, answer wrong. But LeetCode constraints `1 <= forbidden[i].length`, so okay.

Potential issue: If `word` is empty and `forbidden` non-empty, loop empty, res=0. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` is empty, `trie` empty, `isForbidden` returns 0 at first char. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is a prefix of another and the shorter is not first, `isForbidden` returns shorter. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `j = len(word)` not `len(word) + 1`. Yes.

Potential issue: If `j` is updated to `i + truc - 1`, and `truc` is an int. Good.

Potential issue: If `truc` is 0, `if truc` false. Good.

Potential issue: If `truc` is a string (original bug), fixed.

Potential issue: If `c not in t` and we return 0, but what if there is a forbidden word starting at i that is a prefix of the current path but the current char is not in t because the path ended? Then no forbidden word can match, so return 0. Good.

Potential issue: If `c not in t` at a node that has sentinel "end" but no child for c, and the current prefix itself is forbidden, we would have returned earlier when sentinel was encountered. If sentinel not encountered, no forbidden word ends at this prefix, and no child for c means no longer forbidden word matches, so return 0. Good.

Potential issue: If `c not in t` at root, no forbidden word starts with c, return 0. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is a prefix of another and the shorter is not first, `isForbidden` returns shorter. We argued correct. But let's think about if returning shorter could cause j to become too small and thus miss a longer valid substring starting at an earlier start that could have included the shorter forbidden word? Wait if a shorter forbidden word starts at i, any substring starting earlier than i and ending after i+L-1 includes the shorter forbidden word, so invalid. Setting j to i+L-1 (the last index of shorter) means for earlier starts, the window ends before that last index, so it excludes the shorter forbidden word. But what about a substring starting earlier than i that ends before i? It doesn't include i, so it doesn't include the forbidden word. That's fine. What about a substring starting earlier than i and ending between i and i+L-2? It includes part of the shorter forbidden word but not all, so valid. Setting j to i+L-1 allows that. If we set j to the end of a longer forbidden word, we would allow substrings that include the shorter forbidden word fully, which would be invalid. Thus returning shorter is necessary. Good.

Potential issue: If a shorter forbidden word starts at i, and there is an even shorter forbidden word starting at i? Then L is shortest. Good.

Potential issue: If a forbidden word starts at i and is not the shortest but the shortest is not detected because it is not a prefix of the trie path? impossible.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `trie` as a `defaultdict` to simplify. Not needed.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word with a character that is `"end"`? No.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is `"end"` and another is `"e"`, at node for 'e', sentinel "end" and child 'n'. In `isForbidden`, after reading 'e', `if "end" in t` returns 1. But what if the forbidden word is `"end"` and not `"e"`; then node for 'e' has no sentinel, so continues. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is `"e"` and another is `"end"`, and word is `"end"`, the answer is 2. We tested.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is `"e"` and another is `"end"`, and word is `"end"`, but the problem might define "forbidden" as exact substrings not allowed, so "e" forbidden means "end" invalid because it contains "e". Yes.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle `forbidden` as a set for O(1) lookup? Original trie.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains a word that is a prefix of another and the shorter is not first, `isForbidden` returns shorter. We argued correct. But let's test with a case where the shorter forbidden word is not actually contained in a candidate substring because the candidate substring starts at i but ends before the shorter forbidden word's end? If shorter length L, candidate length < L avoids it. So max valid length L-1. Good.

Potential issue: If a forbidden word starts at i and is length L, but there is a valid substring starting at i of length > L that avoids the forbidden word by ending before its start? Impossible because forbidden starts at i. Good.

Potential issue: If a forbidden word starts at i and is length L, but there is a valid substring starting at i of length > L that somehow doesn't include the forbidden word because the forbidden word is not contiguous? It is contiguous by definition. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `res = max(res, j - i)` after updating j. If L found, j-i = L-1. But what if there is a longer valid substring starting at i that ends before j_old but after i+L-1? We said impossible. Good.

Potential issue: If L found and L=1, j=i, res=0. But what if there is a valid substring starting at i of length 0? Empty substring. If empty substrings are considered valid, length 0. Our res can be 0. Good.

Potential issue: If the problem expects at least 1? LeetCode returns 0 if no valid non-empty. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def isForbidden(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            for k in range(start, end):
                c = word[k]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return k - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(i, j)
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
